The shot caught the wraith across what might generously be called its torso — a wound that barely registered on something like this — the only wound anyone managed to land on the thing before he broke for the secret passage. I am told I should credit this. I do. Zephyr wounded the wraith and then ran, which is functionally what the rest of us were about to do as well, so perhaps the lesson is simply that even recklessness improves when pointed at a door.

The wraith was hurt. The wraith was not stopped.

The party began dashing. One by one, boots loud on stone, the Air Temple corridor filling with the very specific sound of adventurers not fighting a tactical retreat but simply *running*. Sequoia. Zinnia. Zephyr already gone. This is the correct response to a wraith that has resisted two consecutive attempts at divine power and announced its intention to kill someone. I knew that.

*But not me*, I explained, to no one in particular, or possibly to St. Cuthbert directly, and I dashed.

I want that on record: plate mail, blessed and magical, the finest protection available to a cleric of my station and general level of being-placed-in-danger-repeatedly. I have good reason to believe I am harder to hit than most.

The wraith reached out as I sprinted and caught me across the back. A blow that cost something real, cold and deep, the kind of cold that doesn't stop at skin — and then a stray bolt of fire from somewhere in the chaos added its own insult on top, because apparently the universe decided one was insufficient. I cleared the threshold. I made it through the passage. I was, technically, alive.

And behind me, echoing up the corridor as we scrambled upstairs, as the stone closed the distance between us and it, came the sound of the wraith laughing at me.

*Hysterically.* It laughed at me *hysterically*.

Two attempts, and nothing to show for either. A divine power that worked on the chimera, that has worked before, that should by every measure of theological logic have worked today — and the wraith's only response to the entirety of my efforts was to chase me into a corridor and laugh.

I am going to need a moment before I speak about this further.

---

## Zephyr — Ambush by Ghouls

The smell arrived before the sound. That sick, wet rot — like meat left in standing water, but with something worse underneath it, something that didn't belong to the natural world at all. We'd pulled back to our own territory to wait out the temple war, and I'd been calculating odds in my head, counting corridors, thinking about Romag and Hartsch and the seventeen ways this whole operation could collapse before morning.

Then that smell.

*Oh god. This again.*

They came out of the dark in a shambling line — four of them, and I recognized the faces. That was the worst part. Not strangers. Not random undead dredged up from some nameless grave. Earth Temple members. People we'd seen in corridors, nodded to while maintaining cover, filed away as *useful* or *obstacle* in the ongoing arithmetic of survival. Now raised by something with necromantic ambitions and sent wandering down here to scavenge whatever the temple war left behind.

"But, aren't they supposed to be, like, fighting upstairs?"

The question hung there, genuinely unanswered, which I found deeply unpleasant. Things were supposed to be fighting upstairs. That was the plan. That was *my* plan. Ghouls lurching into our territory, stinking of fresh death and bad magic, was not in the plan.

Sequoia moved fast — bless him — already working to set something at the door. But the ghouls rolled forward like they had somewhere to be, and whatever trap he was laying, they hit it before it was ready. The mechanism failed at the worst possible moment — the kind of failure that makes you want to reconsider your life choices. The spring went early, the delay was nothing, and the ghouls kept coming.

Fine. Fine.

The nearest one hadn't reached me yet. Hadn't reacted. Was still in that lurching half-moment between shambling forward and noticing there was something in its way with opinions about being devoured. I've been paralyzed by ghouls before (we all have, at this point — the claws hit and your body just *stops*, a sensation I would describe as among my least favourite), so I have a very personal and practical interest in killing them first.

The dagger was already in my hand.

Clean. Precise. The creature dropped where it stood like someone had simply switched it off.

*They attack. They die.* Except this time, they didn't even get the attack.

I was already tracking the other three when Calmer happened.

The man has been knocked unconscious more times than I can count, raised from the dead once, used as bait at least twice — that last one I will own, that was me, and I stand by the tactical reasoning even if the execution had some notes. He has been burned by fire priests and drowned by Thalsor's magic and told to *be ready to die* before a ceremony that I knew would probably try to kill him. He has been patient about this in his own particular way, which is to say he has not been patient at all, and there has been escalating friction, and the concern Zinnia had put into words once, something about Calmer going native, which I am choosing not to look at directly.

None of that mattered in the moment Calmer let the undead have it.

The holy wrath came out of him like something that had been stored under pressure for weeks. It hit the room — all three remaining ghouls unable to hold against it, every last one of them — and they *fled*. Not shambled. Not lurched. Fled in terror, taking the toll as they ran, the divine power chasing them through the door like a creditor who's finally located a defaulter.

I watched it happen. Calmer was going *"yes, yes, yes, yes, yes, yes, yes, yes, yes, yes, yes, yes!"* The cursing started. *This is how the power of—* and then more cursing. The sentence never finished. He didn't need it to.

I watched the war cleric come fully unhinged in the best possible way, and I had two thoughts at once. The first was: *this is what two failed attempts on the wraith looks like when it finally finds a target that breaks before it.* The frustration releasing itself, all of it, through the conduit of divine power and vocabulary I won't repeat.

The second thought was quieter and I didn't linger on it.

The second thought was: *is that him, or is that something else wearing his voice?*

Because Calmer hates undead. Calmer always hated undead. That's not new — a cleric of St. Cuthbert spitting fire at animated corpses is the most theologically coherent thing he does. But the *yes yes yes yes yes* had an edge to it. The way Zinnia described it — the concern he won't say out loud while framing it as purely operational — I've filed it and I look at it sometimes. Two failed attempts on the wraith. The ceremony, Romag's toast, the creeping months inside a temple where Evil is spelled with a capital letter and they keep expecting you to mean it.

Sequoia put an arrow in the back of the last one before it made it out the corridor. Clean finish. That's Sequoia.

The cursing faded. The smell didn't, not entirely. And I looked at the dropped ghoul — my ghoul, killed before it could act, the assassin's precision Zinnia respects and won't say he respects — and then at the door where the others had fled.

"Maybe we should go find that guy?"

Because the ghouls had been Earth Temple members. Recently dead, recently raised. Someone with necromantic power was working in this dungeon right now, during our carefully engineered war, with access to our people's corpses and apparent interest in deploying them.

That was a problem. That was several problems layered on top of each other.

The sounds of Calmer's breathing slowly returned to something resembling normal. I noted it. Filed it. Did not examine it further.

---

## Sequoia — Descent into the Second Level Corridors

The short rest had done what it could, which wasn't much. Calmer's colour had come back. Zephyr was still moving like something inside him wasn't quite right. The sounds beyond our territory hadn't stopped — distant impacts, shouted orders, the kind of echoing commotion that tells you a building is eating itself. We listened to it from borrowed stone floors and called it a rest.

When we moved back through the Air Temple, the wraith was where we'd left it. Same corridor. Same cold. Same hollow patience. Calmer didn't say anything, and neither did I. The wraith didn't move as we cleared its territory. That was worse. We kept moving. I didn't look back when I heard it drift away.

The second level hit different the moment we reached it. Cooler air. The smell of blood and old stone. Broken bottles scattered across the floor like someone had celebrated early and poorly. Armor shoved against the walls — not placed, shoved, the way you clear a path in a hurry. Blood trails running into shadow and stopping nowhere near a body. The war sounds were still there, muffled by stone but present from every direction at once, which meant the fighting wasn't localized anymore.

I was reading the floor before I was reading the room.

That's the habit. Greased hinges, foot traffic, bloodstain direction. These aren't decoration. They're a map of what happened and who survived it. The floor down here was telling me something had broken open — not just a skirmish, something structural. I just didn't have the full picture yet.

Then I saw the goblins.

They were moving through a hidden door that had no business being visible, hauling bags that clinked with the unmistakable weight of coin. Behind them, ghouls. Ghouls. Dragging looted weapons, threading the same passage with the same purposeful efficiency. Nobody scrambling. Nobody panicking. They weren't fleeing the battle.

They were shopping in it.

I knew the badge. Kelno's people. The Air Temple cleric who had sat across from Romag's table like furniture, quiet enough that I'd filed him under *background noise* and moved on. That was a mistake. I was looking at the correction. Kelno had read the board and decided his move was to let the other temples destroy each other while his people walked the corridors with empty bags and filled them.

I thought about how many times we'd been in a room with him and not looked twice.

That's the thing about faction politics — you get fixated on the loud players. Romag's anger. The Fire Temple's fire. The Water Temple's ambition. You forget that the quietest person in the room is often the one who's already decided they don't need to win the argument, just outlast everyone having it.

Zephyr said it out loud, the way Zephyr does: *"So… We've done a good job. Right."*

And it was true. All four temples were in this now.

*"So, perfect for us to go down to the lower temple."*

He wasn't wrong. I watched the last goblin vanish into the passage — one bag, maybe sixty pounds of someone else's gold — and tallied it up. We'd engineered a war between three factions and a fourth had walked into the fire on its own terms. The Obsidian Edge had lit the match and stepped back, and now Kelno was standing in the smoke with a different set of burns and a full treasury.

That was either very good for us or a problem we hadn't accounted for yet.

I wasn't sure which. I filed it under *both* and kept walking.

---

## Zinnia — The Hall of Trophies and Elemental Strife

The bones told a story.

I catalogued them the way I catalogue everything, automatically, without sentiment. The hall had once hosted celebrations, if you could call them that. Great banquets of conquest, probably. The trophies on the walls said as much: mummified heads, their leather-dry mouths still twisted in the expressions of their dying moments. Torn battle standards. Battered shields. A broken broadsword that someone had kept as a trophy, which meant someone had once been proud of breaking it. The tapestries showed raiding — figures in the act of taking, burning, dragging. Broad steps of dull black stone descended from the north wall into shadow, and the silence of the place pressed down like a thumb.

Ruins have a grammar. You can read them if you know how.

This one said: something powerful celebrated here, and now it is gone, and all it left behind were its worst moments.

*Noted.*

---

The heat reached us before the sound did.

Fire snakes and a wounded salamander, locked in combat with four water gargoyles — the collisions between them threw eruptions of scalding steam into the already close air, and the noise was a constant wet violence that didn't resolve into anything intelligible. I watched for a moment, parsing it. Fire versus Water. Neither faction was going to yield. The salamander was wounded but still moving, still contributing to the chaos. The gargoyles showed no sign of flagging.

No tactical objective. No leverage. No way in.

Just the violence itself, filling the chamber with heat and noise because violence, left uninterrupted, continues until it can't.

I was still watching when Zephyr — because of course it was Zephyr — decided he wanted to ask one of them a question.

"I wanna find one guy and ask him where the deep temple is."

The answer was already in the creatures, for anyone watching closely enough to read it. They were not men. They were elemental things, and whatever they spoke was Primordial — a tongue none of us carried.

"Speak goblin," Zephyr offered anyway.

A beat.

Goblin was not going to reach a gargoyle. That was the end of it.

"Alright," Zephyr said. "This plan is done."

I didn't say anything. Whatever knowledge those creatures carried — whatever they might have told us about the deep temple's layout, its factions, its pressure points — it was locked behind a language none of us spoke, inside minds that had no particular interest in us.

A language gap with nothing to translate through. No way in.

---

I had noticed the door earlier. I always notice doors.

This one opened on its own.

Not dramatically — not with a gust of wind or the grinding of ancient mechanisms. Just: open, when it had been closed a moment before. Then closed again. The hall was full of noise from the elemental fight, full of heat and steam, and I was watching it between the billowing clouds as the party debated the uselessness of Goblin as a diplomatic language.

Watching us.

The thought arrived without alarm — I am a professional, and professionals don't waste alarm on hypotheticals. But I catalogued it. A door that moves on its own in a ruined hall is either a draft, a mechanism, or an observer. The hall was otherwise still. Drafts don't aim themselves. Mechanisms don't pause between movements like something checking whether you've noticed.

Someone, or something, was tracking our position.

I filed it. Asset or threat, TBD. Move south. The steam gives cover.

---

That was the exit strategy. No magic, no subtlety required — the elementals were generating enough obscuring haze on their own that slipping away south was simply a matter of patience and timing. We moved between the billows, staying low, staying quiet, letting the heat swallow the sound of our passage.

The salamander's shriek cut through once. Something was losing ground in there.

We didn't stop to find out which side.

The heat clung to my skin as we went, and the sounds of the battle faded behind us into the haze — and I kept the door in my peripheral attention until the geometry of the corridor finally took it from view.

Still watching us, or already forgotten? 

I didn't know. That bothered me more than the elementals.

---

## Sequoia — The Prisoner's Bargain

The chamber smelled like fear and old stone. That particular combination — unwashed people, iron, the faint copper of something that had happened here before we arrived — I've learned to read it the way a sailor reads weather. It means people have been waiting a long time, and they've stopped expecting good news.

They were huddled against the far wall, chained at the wrist, and the moment we came through that door their voices hit us all at once.

*"Please, please don't leave us to die!"*

I took stock without slowing down. Human, all of them. Thin. The kind of thin that isn't about food so much as about sustained terror. They'd been gathered for something, and that something was not going to be pleasant for them. I've seen enough of how this temple thinks to know what prisoners are for here. I didn't let myself think too long about the ones we hadn't found in time.

Zephyr was already moving — he has a nose for information, whatever else you want to say about him. He turned to the group and asked whether anyone knew about the Deep Temple.

Silence from most of them. And then one man, apart from the others slightly, looked up with the particular expression of someone who has been waiting to have something to trade.

*"I know about the Deep Temple,"* he said. *"But I'm only going to talk if you let — if you get me out of here."*

Smart. Or at least, experienced enough to know that information without leverage is just a gift, and people who've been chained in a temple dungeon don't have anything left to give away for free. I didn't blame him.

Zinnia had been watching the man the whole time. I saw him do that thing: the small stillness, the focused attention. He's good at reading people. Better than he lets on, and he already lets on plenty. Whatever he saw, it settled something, because he gave me the look that means: *this one's telling the truth.*

Right. So. The lock.

I stepped forward, crouched down, and got to work. The lock gave cleanly. The shackles came apart like they'd been waiting for me. There's a kind of satisfaction in a clean pick that I'd be embarrassed to admit to anyone. It's just physics, really. Everything has a mechanism. Everything has a point where it opens. You find the point, you apply the right pressure, and the thing gives way. It's the clearest version of how I think the world works.

The chains hit the stone floor.

That was when the others found their voices.

*"What about us, what about us?"*

Four still chained. Frightened. I looked at them, really looked (which I try not to do too long because it makes the arithmetic harder), and thought about how many locks I still had to pick tonight, and what we still needed to do before morning, and whether any of this was going to matter if we didn't get the information that was currently standing in front of us, rubbing his wrists, sizing us up.

Zephyr told them they'd have their turn. Two seconds. I kept my face neutral.

The freed man was doing his own accounting now. He looked at us — Earth Temple badges, weapons, the particular posture of people who've been fighting their way through something — and his expression shifted from desperate to cautious to something almost like wry.

*"The Deep Temple?"* he said. *"What do you want to know about the Deep Temple?"*

*"How do you get there?"* Zephyr asked.

The man actually laughed. Not a warm laugh. The laugh of someone who had been near enough to something terrible to find the question genuinely absurd.

*"Because nobody wants to get to the Deep Temple. Everybody wants to not — not ever be near the Deep Temple, but..."*

Zephyr asked why he was laughing.

I understood why he was laughing. I've been here long enough to understand that some things are funny because the alternative is something you can't look at directly. The laughter stopped when Calmer stepped forward, and the room changed in the way rooms change when Calmer means business. There's something about a priest who's survived being killed once that reads differently than before. The prisoner felt it.

Zephyr had tried persuasion first — I caught the flat delivery, saw it land badly. But with Calmer at his shoulder, he tried again, and the second attempt found its footing.

The man looked at us. He looked at the door. He looked at his own hands, free now and still faintly red from the iron.

*"I guess I would be pushing my luck if I insisted for more help, wouldn't I? My mother never raised — didn't raise me to be a fool."*

He paused, and there was something honest in that pause. A man deciding. Not performing surrender, actually deciding.

*"Alright. So here's the secret door to the Greater Temple."*

He pointed it out on our map. Precise. A hidden passage inside the Air Temple itself, knowledge he'd gotten directly from Kelno — which meant it was the kind of information that doesn't circulate widely, the kind you only know if you were close enough to matter or close enough to overhear.

I noted it. I noted him. Air Temple cultist, which made him a prisoner here for reasons of faction politics as much as anything else. Smart enough to trade. Smart enough to know the shape of what he was dealing with. His mother had raised him right, apparently.

The other four were still chained to the wall.

I had more work to do.

---

## Zephyr — Beckoned

The freed prisoner's directions were good. The door was real, the location confirmed, the path to the Deep Temple laid out in words Zephyr had turned over in his head until they sat flush and solid, like a key he could feel the weight of even when he wasn't touching it.

He'd had to work for those words. Not elegantly.

The interrogation hadn't been his finest hour — he'd leaned on the man harder than necessary, all bravado and borrowed threat, standing at the full height the horns gave him and hoping the prisoner would fill in the rest. The man had. Fear is efficient that way. Zephyr didn't find it satisfying exactly, but satisfying wasn't the point. The point was the information, and now he had it. Another notch in the victory belt, even if the notching had been sloppy.

So. The door existed. They knew where it was.

The moment he turned the location over in his mind — corridor junction, left passage, counting stones from the bracket — was the moment the runner found them.

An Earth Temple runner, collar damp with effort, mouth already open before he'd finished stopping. Hartsch wanted them. Hartsch wanted his Obsidian Edge assembled, upstairs, immediately. War council. The summons had the texture of *now*.

Zephyr looked at the runner, then at the corridor beyond him, then at nothing in particular.

He filed the directions away in the same place he kept everything he planned to collect later: the Rhennee debt, Captain Tolub's ship schedule, the exact weight of every favor he was owed. The Deep Temple wasn't going anywhere. Presumably.

The weight of it was the thing he didn't say aloud. They had it, finally had it, and now they had to go back up. Because Hartsch whistled and the Obsidian Edge came running, because that was the arrangement they had made, because Hartsch was useful and useful things required maintenance. The alliance was real. The leash attached to it was also real.

*This again.* 

Not prejudice this time. Something else. The particular indignity of being someone else's asset while you were trying to build your own balance sheet.

He let the runner see him receiving the message calmly. Unhurried. The kind of man who goes where he chooses, and who happens to be choosing this. He gestured for the others to follow and started walking.

The door's location sat behind his eyes like money he hadn't spent yet.
# Chapter 26 Attrition 

## Zephyr — The Sealed Greater Temple

The goblins were the first thing we saw when we pushed into the Air Temple.

Moving fast toward Kelno's territory, arms piled with everything they'd stripped from the dead. Equipment, robes, the kind of things you take when you're not sure there will be another opportunity. They kept their eyes down. We kept ours forward. Nobody said anything.

*This again.* The war I'd helped arrange, moving around me in looting parties.

The Water Temple door had burn marks on it. The Fire Temple had tried to force an entry and failed, or stopped, or been stopped. Hard to say which. The bodies along the passage didn't clarify matters. Looted already, by the time we got there. Everything useful gone.

The wraith was at the entrance to the Greater Temple when we arrived. Feasting. Not hunting. Feasting, which is a distinction that mattered to my continued good health. He looked up from whatever unfortunate corpse had his attention and called out with the warmth of something that finds everything amusing.

"Your turn will come! Don't worry, my little pets! Your turn will come."

The last time I'd stood this close to him, I'd come away with something missing: not a wound exactly, more like a portion of myself that the cold reached into and found worth keeping. That feeling has not entirely left.

Sequoia said, "Lucky me."

I agreed with the sentiment.

Calmer, naturally, saw an opportunity.

"I can kill him! I can kill him, I can so totally kill him!"

"It's okay," I said.

I meant: *the wraith knows things.* Calmer apparently heard the first word and not the rest, because he subsided, still looking at the wraith with that hungry ecclesiastical expression he gets when he's found something worth smiting. I appreciate the energy. I have learned not to rely on it as a tactical plan.

We pressed on toward the Greater Temple entrance. I had, in point of fact, never been through.

"Have you gone through the Greater Temple?" I asked.

No one had.

"Oh, we could go there."

Someone agreed. We moved.

The door didn't cooperate. Something held it from the other side: braced, or barricaded, or committed to not opening. "Or dash through," I said. Calmer began hammering at it instead, which achieved the same noise with considerably less tactical elegance, and the wraith found this very funny.

I turned back to face him.

The wraith had been here the whole time. Watching everything we'd done in this temple: the dead we'd made, the war we'd triggered, the bodies lining every passage we'd walked through. Information was information, and the wraith was currently the only entity in the room willing to talk.

"And Gary Race," I said, "can you tell us what's happening?"

He looked at me with the amusement of something that has eaten recently and is deciding whether to eat again.

"I could tell you," he said, "or I could kill you—"

He paused. His attention drifted to the bodies around him, the ones we'd arranged, directly or otherwise.

"But you provided me so much tasty food! Food!"

Then: "I want to thank you about something."

"Yes," I said.

I let it run. The wraith wanted to be thanked. It had been alone in this corridor for weeks, feasting on whatever the war left behind, with nobody willing to call it by name and ask it what it knew. And here was someone doing exactly that. I gave it that. I could feel the room tilt: the wraith no longer performing menace but something closer to collegial warmth. Peer to peer. I'd given it an audience. The price of the audience was conversation, and conversation was exactly what I'd come for.

"With all the bodies you've created," he announced, with the satisfaction of a man presenting a ledger that has resolved favorably, "I now have minions."

The specters materialized one by one. He counted them out. One, two, three. Four, five. Mostly Earth Temple forces. I recognized the remnants of robes, the insignia, the general silhouette of people we had, in one way or another, contributed to killing. Six, seven.

Seven specters. Raised from bodies we were responsible for.

*Good,* I thought. *The wraith is building something. We need to make sure it builds it for us.*

"Why don't you go fight the other temples?" I said.

He did not answer. He was busy being pleased with himself.

Then the voice.

Cold. From behind the door Calmer had been hammering. Every one of us stopped where we stood.

"The Greater Temple is sealed until the battle is over. No one gets in, no one gets out."

The wraith laughed harder.

"Where are you?" I asked the door.

The wraith answered instead.

"You see, what you don't understand, you poor little fools, you poor little delightful little fools — this happens every so often, and when it does, the Greater Temple seals the lesser temples and waits for the battle to end. You're going to be trapped in here like the rest of us, and maybe you'll be like one of my servants."

He said "my servants" with obvious satisfaction. He was counting on it.

I let it settle. We were inside. The door was sealed. The wraith had seven specters and a month's supply of Earth Temple dead to expand the collection. The voice on the other side had decided the war was not yet over.

"Who is… what is the Greater Temple?" I asked.

"The Greater Temple," the wraith said, with the tone of something explaining things it finds obvious, "is the deep temple. It's the people who are really in charge."

The people who are really in charge.

I'd been wrong about who sat at the top before. Romag. Then Hartsch. Then us, for a moment. Every time I thought I'd found the ceiling, another one appeared above it.

"I'd like to meet them," I said.

The voice from behind the door answered.

"Survive the fight, and maybe you'll get a chance."

Terms. Don't die in the war we started, and there would be an introduction. I've worked with worse opening offers.

I looked at the seven specters. I looked at the wraith. I looked at the door.

"Why don't we camp out next door?" I said.

Nobody moved toward camping.

The seven specters were, as it turned out, a relevant consideration. The wraith had them and was not going to stop having them. The door was sealed. We were, by the wraith's own enthusiastic assessment, excellent raw material for future servants.

"Alright," I said. "We'll get out and come back later."

The wraith shouted after us as we moved.

"I can't wait till you come back here! I will be waiting!"

He meant it warmly. The absolute confidence of something that has already decided the outcome and is merely permitting time to catch up.

The tiefling kept walking.

*Survive the war. The door opens when the battle ends. We'll be on the right side of it.*

## 

## Zinnia — Return to the Earth Temple

The arrowheads were the first thing I saw.

Two positions, six guards, all of them reading our emergence from the stairwell as a threat. Which, given the state of the second level, was a reasonable assessment. The party had been gone. Things had not gone well. The gap between our departure and our return had been filled with sounds they couldn't account for and a body count they couldn't afford.

Their leader got his voice out before his men got their fingers to the triggers.

"STOP! Stop! Don't fight!"

Then the recognition. Then the question.

"Where the hell have you been?"

Zephyr gave him the short version. "Uh, we've been fighting."

"Where?"

"We opened the door for you."

It was enough to get us moving and not enough to get us trusted. That gap was still open. The leader took it, set it somewhere between "acceptable" and "later," and redirected us.

"Yeah, well… well, after you open the door, you guys disappeared. Things have not gone well. Hartsch needs you right now. Go to the temple."

"We're going," Zephyr said.

We went.

---

I counted the soldiers before I looked at Hartsch.

The number was wrong. Not collapse-of-the-faction wrong, but wrong enough that I counted them twice before I trusted what I was seeing. Whatever had happened while we were on the second level had cost the Earth Temple more than it should have for a faction holding its own ground. These were men who had started this war expecting to win it quickly. The quick part was behind them.

Then I looked at Hartsch.

Exhausted. Dirty. Bloodied in ways that suggested proximity to the fighting, not command distance from it. He had been in it, or near enough. The muffled boom of something two levels below, or in a corridor we weren't in, settled into the stone under our feet and went quiet.

Hartsch looked at us.

"What do we do next?"

Four words. The authority transfer was complete.

I had watched this happen with other commanders, on other operations: the moment when a man who has been pretending to be in charge finally stops pretending. Hartsch had held the Earth Temple together through a three-way war he didn't fully understand, with forces he was losing, and now he was asking us. The Obsidian Edge. The people who had engineered this situation from underneath him.

*Information he doesn't get to have. We built this; he inherited it.*

Zephyr took it first.

"We wait it out."

Hartsch stared at him. "What do you mean we waited out?"

"So… we'll wait till the other temples stop fighting. Just to… okay, because… but right now, it's not the best time to go on the offense."

Hartsch's voice went sharp.

"But that… but that is Romarg's passive strategy!"

There it was. A name that stung him, attached to a strategy that shamed him, and Zephyr had just handed it back as a reasonable plan. I caught it. Hartsch would rather lose aggressively than win the way that man had. Whatever it cost him.

That was leverage. I didn't say so.

Zephyr pivoted. "Hmm… well, the situation is evolving."

"What do you mean, the situation is evolving?"

"Well, you can go to the Obsidian Edge and… we'll hold on for it here with a couple of troops."

Hartsch looked at him. "Is that wise? For me to go on the vanguard?"

"Well, take half the troops, we'll stay here with the other half. We can't leave our territory undefended."

He was past strategy now. I could see it in his hands. The questions weren't tactical. He didn't know where to stand anymore.

"Where should I put the troops? It's all gone to hell!"

"No, it's fine, it's fine. Just go… go fight the… the water in the fire temple. I'll hold down for it here. You got it."

Zephyr was improvising. I could see it register: the soldiers' eyes going to each other instead of to him. I looked at the soldiers around us. Bloodied. Fewer than before. This was supposed to have been an easy win, and they knew whose faction had started the war. They were watching us, the Obsidian Edge, waiting to see if we were about to disappear on them again.

"Alright, we will leave the vanguard."

Hartsch's shoulders changed.

"So, uh, we'll charge once more to the Water Temple!"

"Yes, let's go."

But Hartsch wasn't done. "So, should we… should we deploy any of our forces to defend anything, or should we just, like, charge straight ahead? Like… is there anything we should do to protect anything?"

"No. Come, everyone in charge, we will make a final stand. It's time we got it to Temple."

The planning found its footing. Someone put the question about relative strength: who was winning, who was weakest, where to push.

"Who's the strongest? The Water Temple. The weakest is the Air Temple. The next is the Fire Temple."

Zephyr absorbed this and turned to us.

"Okay, here's the plan, guys. We'll go after the Air Temple. Take all their treasure. And from there, we'll see if we attack that water."

It was the right call. I had read the same conclusion in Hartsch's face when he listed the order. He had not said it with pride. He had listed it the way a man lists damage he's already accepted, in order, without argument, like reading something he already knew.

Hartsch looked around the room. Something in him settled. Not confidence. Something close enough to move on.

"Very well! This is the most heavily defended part of the territory. We will stay here for the night, and we will attack again."

We rested.

The sounds reached us from corridors we weren't in. The war was running on its own momentum. Good. It didn't need us to keep burning.

---

## sequoia — War Council and Hartsch's Blunder

The war room smelled like men who hadn't slept. Hartsch was in the middle of it, asking what to do next like a man who'd never once had to answer that question himself.

Zephyr said wait. Let the temples bleed each other out. It was the correct answer, the cheap answer, the one that cost us nothing.

Hartsch didn't like it. He brought up Romag: what Romag would have wanted, what Romag built, all of it in the voice of a man reaching for borrowed authority. We'd killed Romag. I didn't say that. Nobody in the room knew it but us, and there is no need for him to know.

So we asked which temple was weakest. Air. Thin guard, thinner walls. Zephyr proposed we hit it, and Hartsch agreed too fast. Agreement was the only thing he could produce reliably. Then he asked for the night to sort deployment.

That was fine. That was the part where a leader sleeps on it and wakes up with orders.

We didn't sleep in the main temple. Zephyr wanted the Romag room. *The special room. Because we're apostles, apparently.* The dead man's quarters, the one place in this building nobody would think to bother us. I watched Hartsch hand the whole question of where the apostles slept off to Zephyr, and Zephyr hand the question of deployment back to Hartsch, *you're the leader, you should be able to do this*, and I thought: nobody in this room wants to hold the thing they're holding.

*Are we sure they're still alive?* I meant the Broken Blades. The men we'd recruited. The men whose lives were now sitting in the hands of a panicking quartermaster who couldn't decide where to put a chair.

Hartsch asked to sleep on it. We let him. We went to the dead man's room.

In the morning he was gone.

I knew before we found the note. An empty office in a building this nervous isn't a man stepping out for air. *There you go.* I'd half expected it. Being half-right is still wrong.

The note was on his desk in his own hand. *I couldn't wait! I sent the Broken Blades to attack!*

I read it twice. Not because it was hard to read. Because I recognized it.

I know that man. I've redistributed treasure when nobody was looking. I've taken quests without asking the others. I've decided, on my own, in the dark, that I knew better than the people standing next to me. *I couldn't wait* is a sentence I have lived inside of. The difference I tell myself is the consequence. I don't know if it's true.

Hartsch was the version of me with no eye for consequence and no patience and a war to play with. We'd put him at the top of the Earth Temple because he was pliable. Pliable is just another word for a man who'll do anything as long as someone else seems to want it, including invent the someone.

*That's odd.* No. *That's not good.*

Back at the temple there were fewer people standing in it than the night before. Empty spaces where guards had been. An attack in the night, and it had cost us more than we could spare. Hartsch confessed it in pieces: he'd sent the Blades down to stage a raid on the Water Temple, and heard nothing back, and now if we lost them we'd lose the guards too, and *you have to do something.*

Zephyr slapped him. Walked up and slapped the panic right across his face. It didn't take. *What do you want me to do, what do you want me to do, what do you want me to do*, the same three words on a loop. He was stuck in the question.

Then Zinnia looked at the map where he'd marked his glorious initiative, and looked at him, and got the rest of it. I watched his face go still the way it does when the lie underneath finishes coming up.

He hadn't just sent them without asking.

He'd told them the orders came from us.

There it was. The whole man, in one move. He'd made the unilateral call and then pinned the name to it that wasn't his, ours, so that when the Blades died in the water it would be the Obsidian Edge that sent them. I'd have admired the instinct if it weren't aimed at me. A small man caught in a big machine, reaching for the only lever he understood: someone else's authority.

So the Air Temple was off. You don't run a two-pronged raid when one prong is already pinned and bleeding under the wrong banner.

"Okay, so we'll go straight to the Broken Blades," Zephyr said.

Use them as the distraction. Let Hartsch advance from the east, if Hartsch could find east. Go pull our men out of the water before the lie killed them.

The plan had switched. It switches. You don't spiral. You pivot.

We went to get them back.

---

## zinnia — The Failed Raid and Rescuing the Broken Blades

The wraith was where it always was, at the choke point, seven specters in a line behind it. The same choke point it had always been, better staffed. Necrotic cold sat in the corridor ahead of us, the kind that doesn't pass when the thing that makes it moves on. We had abandoned the Air Temple assault to come back here. Operationally, that was a problem for later. Right now there was a dead thing between us and the men we'd sent to die.

Zephyr didn't slow down. "I don't have time for this one," he told it.

The wraith found that amusing. *What do I get in exchange?* It looked us over and offered terms: live bodies, or intelligence. *Tell me where the next attack is coming so I can feast on the dead.* A merchant. Even here, even dead, everyone is running an angle.

"Water temple," Zephyr said. No hesitation. Zephyr gave up Water Temple dead in exchange for passage. The wraith got what it wanted. We got through. I noted what it now knew about us.

The thing deflated. *Oh, okay, that wasn't very exciting. Feasted on them enough. I was hoping for some Earth Temple blood.* I marked it. A wraith with a grudge against the faction we wear the badges of is leverage, and it is also a threat, and the two are not separable. Zephyr told it its turn would come. It smiled, and it let us pass, and I watched it calculate the whole way through. *It remembers we came past. It remembers we paid in someone else's people.* Good intelligence and bad debt, in the same transaction.

Then the door, and behind the door, the slaughterhouse.

Dren Halveth had me by the arm before I'd cleared the threshold. Battered, bloodied, his eyes those of a man who'd already counted his dead twice and gotten the same number. Behind him the survivors stood, hands slick to the wrist, staring at nothing. The stench of opened flesh filled the whole chamber. These were the Broken Blades. We had recruited them. Dren had liked Sequoia. The man who liked Sequoia and the man standing in this doorway were the same person with a lot of dead between them.

"You didn't say it would be a slaughterhouse!" he said. "Romag's replacement, Hartsch is a mad man! We're being fed to a meat grinder."

"It was not us who sent you here, but Hartsch," Zephyr said. "However, we are still deeply saddened by your — whatever happened here."

*Hartsch.* I let the name sit. He'll be dealt with. That wasn't reassurance for them. It was a note to myself, the kind you don't say out loud because saying it spends it. He's our nominal superior and he's a liability and the day is coming when those two facts resolve.

But Dren needed something now, and condolences weren't going to hold these men together. They were one bad word from breaking and running into the corridor we'd just bargained our way through, straight into the thing that wanted Earth Temple blood. I don't rally people. I observe, I wait, I move. This was the other thing, the thing I almost never reach for, because it can't be faked and faking it is all I do.

I stepped in front of them. Gore-soaked, hollow-eyed, finished men. I'd showed up. That mattered more than anything I could say. I'd walked in personally, past the wraith, into the room nobody sane would enter. So I said the true thing.

"I don't know what your plans are. But whatever your plans are, you are with us. We are with you."

I knew it the moment it left me. The exact word. I could see it land. Something behind Dren's eyes came back. The men straightened. They looked at me and gave it back, the same words, a pledge: *whatever your plans are, you are with us. We are with you.* Not loyalty to an order. Loyalty to the person who showed up when the order left them there. I don't do this often. When I do, it lands.

Zephyr moved them out. "Come backwards, we'll hold the line for now." Fall back to the Earth Temple, rest there, but if they were true warriors, he told them, they could stay with us. Half a retreat, half a recruitment. He was already recruiting. He never leaves a room without adding to the count.

They saw the problem before we named it: the wraith blocked the only exit, seven specters at its side, a choke point that would keep killing them. "Alright, we'll deal with this, Dren," Zephyr said.

The blades looked to Calmer. "Do you need our help, boss?"

"I don't need any help to deal with the undead," Calmer said. Then, quieter, the hint pitched at us and not at them: it would be better if they weren't here to see him call on St. Cuthbert's power. *Operational security.* Even now, even with these men pledging themselves to us, the one true thing about him is the thing he can't let witnesses see.

"Alright, calm," Zephyr said. "Let's go."

---

## calmer — Battle with the Wraith and Specters

I had laid the ground before I crossed the threshold. A weapon of conjured light hung at my shoulder, a thing of judgment that moved when I willed it. And the chamber itself, I filled it. A golden aura of summoned spirits, the radiance of a power I have not said aloud in this place in longer than I want to count, pooling across the brown marble and its veins of black, waiting for something dead to step into it. To any wretched, hollow thing that should not exist in the world, that aura is fire. To me it is the warmest thing I have felt down here.

So I charged in. Boldly. I want that recorded.

And the Wraith, the one that had laughed at me twice, that had stood between us and the way down for too long, turned its empty face and said, almost delighted, "Oh, you're back!"

"Uh, yeah," Zephyr said.

Then the room moved.

Sequoia hit a specter and it came apart, gone before it understood it was dying. Zephyr stepped into a second one, his blade finding the seam before the thing had so much as twitched, and it unraveled. He drifted to a third and clipped it, a glancing thing, nothing fatal, but their numbers were already falling in our favor and I let myself believe, for one breath, that this would be clean.

It was not going to be clean.

The Wraith flew. It crossed into my golden field and the radiance bit into it. I felt the working strain and pull, saw it stagger and slow in the light. Good. *Good.* And then it reached me anyway.

I have been hurt before. This was not that. This was cold pouring in where warmth should be: not a wound, an absence, something reaching past the plate and the prayers and taking. Not heat applied. Heat *removed.* I felt the floor of myself drop out and knew, instantly and completely, that I was at the very bottom of what a man can hold and still stand. And worse, I knew this particular cold does not mend. No light I carry would fill that back in. I was going to walk the rest of this with the door open.

Something else came after the cold, some second clawing thing trying to follow the first into me. And it didn't. I held. Clean, complete, the kind of holding that surprises even the one doing it. I shut the door in its face and it did not get through.

The specters swarmed then, three of them grasping at me, and missed, every one, and paid the toll for being in my light: the gold searing them as they reached. One lost its nerve and tried to flee the aura. *No.* I caught it on the way out, before I could think it through, the answer already leaving me, the old draconic gift flaring in my chest, a strength it could not meet, a fist of force that flung it back broken into the radiance it was running from. Zinnia finished it before it could try again.

I was at the very bottom of myself, one touch from gone, and I knew it. I want that recorded too, because of what I did next.

I did not heal. I did not run. I am a warrior of St. Cuthbert and we do not run from the dead. And besides, there was nothing left in me that could undo what the Wraith had taken. So I reached for the thing I had reached for twice before, in two other rooms, on two other nights, and had it die in my hands both times. The creature had *laughed.* The rite of turning, the call that breaks the dead. The third time.

I called it. The light went out of me in a single blast of judgment, and the room of dead things flinched as one. And they could not hold. The Wraith could not hold. Specter after specter could not hold. They came apart in the radiance, all of them but one. Four nights of carrying this, and they just came apart.

The Wraith was dead. The thing that had mocked me. *Dead.*

I could not contain it. I did not try.

"It worked! It worked!" The words came out of me without permission, without dignity, four nights of it failing in my hands and now this. "Who's the man? Who is the man?"

"That's you," Zephyr said.

Zinnia put down the last surviving specter: missed the first strike, took it on the second, and when it still refused, still clinging to the last of itself, he flowed into another and it was simply over.

The passage was clear. No more cold thing standing guard over the way down, no more creature that murders your friends and stands them back up as itself. Two roads to the second level now, and one of them open. A real victory, the strategic kind, the kind Hartsch's war wanted.

I stood in the cooling gold with the door still open in my chest, unable to be healed past the bottom of myself, and I was, against all sense, grinning.

---

## sequoia — The Defector's End

We were turning to go up. First level, the war council, Hartsch waiting on us like we owed him something. Calmer had just put the wraith down and had nothing left in him. I could see it in how he stood, all the color gone, nothing left.

Then movement. Down the corridor, against the brown marble, a bugbear stumbling toward us with a sack of coins, both arms around it. Babbling. Kelno had promised him safety. Kelno had promised him gold.

Zephyr read it wrong first. "So… You're for the water temple."

No. Not water. I watched the understanding change on his face the same time it changed on mine. This one was ours.

"Where are you from?" Zephyr pushed. The answer came out of the babbling. Earth Temple. *Our* wall. One of the bugbears who was supposed to be standing between us and the things that wanted us dead, and he was walking out with somebody else's money in his hands.

"And you're leaving?" Zephyr again. Then, when that didn't land: "Okay. Remember who's the most powerful temple, bugbear?"

Calmer didn't wait for the appeal to work. He came in low and loud with next to nothing left in him, a breath from collapse, the wraith's cold still in his chest, and he said, "Don't mess with me, man! I just killed the Wraith!" Then, louder: "This is how the earth temple rules, with power and force!" He was getting into it. Too into it. Somewhere under the elemental cult armor the cleric was supposed to be wearing a costume, and the costume had started doing the talking. Not my problem right now.

The question came around to me. Chase him. Intimidate him. Threaten him. Find out what's going on.

"I think he's okay."

I didn't mean it the way it sounded. I meant the situation was clear, the read was finished, there was nothing here that needed working out. He was taking money from Kelno to change sides. That's the whole of it. A man who sells the wall he's standing on doesn't get a second wall.

"Uh, we're gonna go kill him, Ben."

I said the rest of it before anyone had moved, before he'd even understood it was happening. "One does not simply take bribes to defect." Because that's the whole reckoning of it. You can be bought. Everyone can be bought. But the buying happens before you take the post, not after, once you're inside the wall you're inside it, and the only exit anyone's going to honor is the one that doesn't have you walking through it.

Good thing I am small. He never clocked me coming.

The first cut found exactly where it needed to go. Clean. He never even turned. It took most of him in the one exchange. He was still standing. Barely. I had one more in me and there was no reason to save it.

He dies.

I stood over the body. "One does not simply take bribes to defect."

Then the part that actually mattered. I went through the sack: twenty gold, and I turned a coin in the light because something was bugging me about the weight of it. The stamp wasn't Kelno's. Wasn't water at all. Air Temple insignia, pressed clean into every piece.

*That's odd.*

*That's not good.*

Kelno hadn't bought him. Somebody upstairs had let us believe Kelno was the one pulling at our bugbears, and the real hand was a different one entirely. The gnolls came out of the wall to prove it, a secret door I hadn't found yet, which meant they'd been close enough to watch the whole thing, furious, talking about money. "We paid good money for that! Now we will get our money, and get food!"

"Didn't know you guys liked eating while dead, but okay."

Air Temple. Kelno had been recruiting, busily, badly, reaching over the Earth Temple's wall and peeling people off one frightened bugbear at a time. We engineered a three-way war and then went and stood at the top of one of the three sides, and the other two had noticed, and this was what noticing looked like: gold with the wrong stamp on it, in the hands of a man who used to guard our wall.

I pocketed the coins. Evidence and payment both. No sense leaving them for the gnolls.

---

## zephyr — Ambush of the Air Temple Gnolls

The bugbear was still settling into the floor when the secret door coughed up its replacements.

Three gnolls, hunched and reeking, spilling out into the corridor with the indignation of creditors. *"We paid good money for that!"* one of them snarled, and I understood the whole situation before the second sentence arrived. *"Now we will get our money, and get food!"* The bugbear had been a purchased defection. We had killed their investment. And now they wanted a refund and a meal, in that order, and they had come to collect both from the people standing over the corpse.

Air Temple gnolls. Kelno's recruitment drive, apparently, running door to door. *This again.* Everyone in this temple was buying everyone else, and the loyalty lasted exactly as long as the coin was warm.

"Didn't know you guys liked eating while dead, but okay," Sequoia said, no setup, no emphasis, and I will admit the line was clean enough that I let it stand. He has been getting funnier the deeper into this place we go. It's the only thing in here that's improving.

Then Dren stepped up with his two mercenaries and put his hands on Calmer.

The cleric did not take it gracefully. *"Unhand me, you creatures!"* Bellowed, full-throated, the over-eager zealot's outrage at being denied a fight he had no business standing in. But the man had nothing left in him. Necrotic cold from the wraith still sat in his chest, and whatever well he draws his healing from was scraped dry; he could not patch himself past the thin margin he was running on. So Dren dragged him back up the corridor, all protest and no leverage. I let it happen. At three hit points the cleric was a resource I couldn't use; Dren was doing us a favor. The balance of the skirmish rearranged itself around his absence.

Three of us now. No healer. Whatever finished these gnolls had to come out of me and Sequoia, and the body knew it before the thought did.

We moved first. We always do: these things never see us coming until we're already inside their reach, and the gnolls were too busy salivating to set themselves.

One of them turned his yellow eyes on me. *"Tiefling flesh!"* it howled, delighted, like a man discovering a delicacy he'd thought extinct. *"Haven't had tiefling flesh in ages!"*

There it was. The world charging me for existing again, except this one wanted to do it with its teeth.

"Come and get it," I said.

I didn't flinch. I never do, not for this. A dryad once asked if I'd crawled out of the Abyss to torment her; a gnome guard once asked *or whatever you are*; and now a starving Air Temple dog wanted me on a spit. Different mouths, same bill. The threat gets solved the same way every time.

I went in on the first one. The opening swing didn't land the way I wanted, sometimes the blade just doesn't fall true, but the second found the gap, and the dagger went where daggers go. The strike from concealment is the only blow that matters. Thud. Steel through the soft place under the ribs, the full weight of the ambush behind it, and the thing folded most of the way to dead in a single beat. Zinnia drifted in and finished what I'd left twitching. One gone.

The survivor got his one good moment then. Caught me across the side, hard enough to register, not hard enough to mean anything. *Not lethal.* The other one lunged and missed entirely. Two dogs, one scratch between them. That's the going rate when you let me set the terms.

Sequoia took the second. Stepped in, tagged it clean, opened it up. The thing had nothing left to lose and lost it anyway.

Which left the last one for me.

Sequoia slid in beside it to give me the angle, and I took the angle. Dagger high, dagger low, then the third from the blind side with everything behind it. Thud. The gnoll didn't so much die as stop. They attack. They die. That's the only eulogy they earn.

And then it was quiet in that ugly brown-and-black corridor, and three of us were standing, and the secret door hung open on whatever the gnolls had crawled out of.

"This leads to the water temple?" I said, already counting what might be behind it.

Air Temple, came the correction. Of course. A whole new route, and we owned the doorway now.

"Well, I think we can go in." But Sequoia was right and I knew it before he said it: no Calmer, no healer, three people and a door into someone else's stronghold. We needed him out front for whatever was in there. He absorbs what the rest of us can't afford to absorb. Without him that job doesn't get done.

"We'll have to go without him." Then, looking at the dark on the far side of it, I corrected myself. Not an assault. Not yet. "Recon mission."

We have ridiculous stealth. A door is just a door when you're the thing nobody hears coming.
# Chapter 27 Dungeon Boss

## calmer — The Aftermath and the Secret Door

The wraith was dead. I held onto that. Whatever else was happening to me, whatever cold had crawled into my chest and stayed there, the wraith was dead and I had been the one to kill it. Twice it had laughed at the holy power I called down in His name. Twice the holy radiance had hit the wraith and simply ceased — the way divine light ceases when it finds nothing that recognizes it, and twice I had felt the outrage of it — the warrior of St. Cuthbert who could not turn a thing that should not exist. And then, on my feet by the grace of nothing but stubbornness, with nothing left in me but reflex and the body's last argument against lying down, I had called the light one final time and it had answered. The chamber went white. The wraith came apart.

*Who's the man,* I thought now, somewhere far down under the cold. *Who is the man.*

The man was being carried.

Hands had me. Many hands, rough hands, the wrong hands — Dren's people, hauling me up off the marble by the arms and the plate, my heels dragging across the brown stone with its black veins, my mace knocking against someone's shin. I tried to tell them. I think I tried. *Unhand me, you creatures.* The words came out, or didn't. The corridor tilted. They were taking me up, away from the stairs, away from the dark passage where the work still was, because — I understood this dimly, the way you understand a thing you would never agree to if you were awake — because they had been told to keep the boss from getting himself killed.

I am the boss. Yes, I am. And the boss does not run from evil.

But they carried me anyway, and the temple slid sideways, and the voices started coming through the floor.

*"You were about to enter the Earth Temple."* That was right. We were. I was. The secret door the gnolls had cracked open for us, the one we'd marked weeks ago — *"Discovered it a while ago."* Yes. I knew that door. I should be at that door.

*"Who wants to lead here?"*

Me. I wanted to lead. I always —

*"My lead,"* said Zephyr, and the cold turned over in my chest.

Of course. His lead. It is always his lead. I built a whole argument once about what my opinions were worth and he watched me drift through it the way he watches me now, waiting to see how much of it I still believed. *Be ready to die,* he'd told me, in that tone, and the wraith had agreed with him — *one of you is going to die today* — and here I was being dragged out of the fight feet-first while the rest of them went down without me.

The voices kept coming. *Stairs leading down. A passageway to the northeast. The lower temple, also probably barricaded.* The Greater Temple had pulled its doors shut and let the lesser ones go to war in the dark, and our whole holy purpose now was to do nothing and stab the survivors. *Let them keep fighting and periodically stab one in the back, and the battle ends.* I would have said something pompous about that. I had read about this just a few moments ago. Well — I hadn't read about it. But I would have made it sound like I had.

*"This time, calmer,"* Zephyr said. *Who's in front? This time, Calmer.*

And I surged — I swear I came up off the dream-floor and reached for the front of the line, *yes, yes, yes, yes, finally* — and the corridor folded shut around me like a hand.

*Calmer is not with you guys. It's just a dream.*

Not with them. Just a dream. Just the cold talking, just the bad audio of a body being hauled up a flight of stairs while the people who matter walk down into the part of the temple where everything happens. I was not in front. I was not even in the room. They were going through the door and I was going up.

Somewhere behind me, or below me, one of Dren's mercenaries fell in to follow them — orders to protect the boss, except the boss was the one being removed, so the boss was me, and I was elsewhere. They'd leave the man at the doorway to watch the rear. He'd be no use deeper in. The mercenaries are anything but stealthy, all clatter and breathing, and Zephyr would lead, and Zinnia and Sequoia would follow, quiet, into the dark, into the Air Temple, into the work — and I would be upstairs, warm and safe and useless, the prophet of stone wrapped in a blanket by men who thought they were doing me a kindness.

*One of you is going to die today.*

The cold sat in my chest and did not leave.

*But not me.*

I didn't even believe it. That was the worst part. I said it to the dark anyway, the way I'd been saying His name to the dark for weeks now — quiet, where no one could hear, where it couldn't blow my cover — and then the stairs took me up and the voices went under the floor and were gone.

---

## zephyr — A Bargain in the Air Temple

Calmer wasn't with us. Deeply injured, parked with Dren's mercenaries somewhere behind a curtain so he wouldn't get himself killed reaching for a god he can't name in here. Fine. I had the others. And I had the only thing in this temple that ever told me the truth: four starving prophets, all chaotic, all hungry for the same chair, and the simplest way to win a war between starving men is to let them eat each other and stab whoever's still chewing. Let them slaughter each other. That wasn't a bad outcome. That was the outcome.

"Let's go export," I said, which is not how the word goes, but nobody corrected me, and we went.

The southwest passage gave us a curtain. I peeked. Someone was sitting in a chair in a room the color of old ash, beckoning like he'd ordered us off a menu.

"Come, sit down, I've been waiting for you."

"Sit down," I said, which made no sense as a reply, and I sat anyway.

Everything gray. Carpet, plaster, hangings, two upholstered chairs, a crock half-full of mead, lamps of dull metal throwing barely enough light to see by. And on the shelves between the scrolls and the foodstuffs — skulls. Human, demi-human, arranged like a man arranges things he's stopped thinking about. A narrow bed in the corner. *I've heard of worse in the Abyss. But this is the room of someone who plans to be alive in the morning.*

"I am Kelno."

"Hello, Kelno," said Sequoia.

Old. Older than any cultist has a right to get. Tired in the bones and cunning behind the eyes, the two things sitting together because one had bought the other. He tapped a sheet of paper. Assassins had reached Hartsch's antechamber an hour ago. By tonight our puppet would be meat. Alrrem was already on his pyre. That left him, and Belsornig, and — most interestingly — us.

He asked for a reaction. We gave him none.

"Yeah," Sequoia said. "We sit there silently and menacingly."

He appreciated the menace. Then he laid it out, and I felt the terms shift as he talked — the weight on each side of the deal rearranging itself without him raising his voice.

When the war ended, the great doors would open, and whoever stood on top of the upper temple became the Greater Temple's new partner. *That is going to be you. I have noted for a week.* I could kill him — worst guards in the dungeon, he admitted it himself — and then walk into the deep temple blind, no goblin eyes, no informants, no bribery network. Or I could walk out with him as my number two. His goblins, mine. His informants, mine. The throne, mine. His little office, his.

"Y'all wanna take the deal?" Sequoia asked the room.

"We have to be suspicious of him," Zinnia said.

She was right. She has a way of finding the part of a thing that doesn't hold — the place where it was already going to give. But I wanted to hear him say more first.

He showed us the note he kept tapping. Belsornig — *by the cannon of the crushing wave*, the man liked the name — the assassination plot against Hartsch, two men through the north service hall, the bar that should've been left unset. And at the bottom, the line that made the whole performance honest: *If the obsidian edge appear in your hall, withdraw to the steam corridors. Do not engage. They are not to be killed.*

"Are you trying to kill us?" Sequoia asked.

He wasn't. That was the enemy's note. Even the man trying to murder our puppet had given orders to leave us breathing. *Can it be that we've become something a man steps around?*

"Kelno," I said. "So — are you for or against? The pimple." My mouth tripped over the word and I let it.

He smiled and gave me nothing. Loyalty to the power of air, he said, the way a man swears loyalty to weather.

"So you're — you're for Chaos, or — for order."

"Yes, I am for chaos, but I'm also for living."

*There it is.* That was the genuine article. Not loyalty — appetite, the clean honest hunger of a man who only wants to wake up tomorrow. I respect that. I trust it more than I'd ever trust a vow.

He asked if anyone had explained what starting a civil war actually meant. They hadn't. I hadn't known there were rules.

"What happens when a conflict ends?" I asked.

The doors open. Whoever's on top leads the lesser temples.

"And who won the last competition?"

"That would have been the former head of the Earth Temple."

I sat in the gray light with the skulls watching and let it land.

"Okay," I said. "So we must align ourselves with the victories."

"We are the victors," Sequoia said.

And he was right, and that was the thing I'd been circling without letting myself touch. We let Hartsch get killed. We let Romag get killed. We had a cleric pretending to be an earth cultist, which made us the fourth group, which meant the chair at the top was already ours — we just hadn't sat down. *This is starting to be a very lucrative operation.*

"Okay, let's — let's make this — let's do that plan."

Belsornig. Yes.

"Very well," Kelno said.

He pointed us toward the Water Temple — lightly guarded now, he observed, as good a time as any to strike.

"We'll get, I cut up the mercenaries," I said. "Yes, I'll take the deal. We won't kill you for his news."

"I think this is the beginning of a wonderful relationship."

I let him have that. Then, because a new partner is owed a formality: "You know, as a formality, since you're a new partner, I just wanna know — do you have anything hiding around here?"

A ledger. Payments out to a dozen names. And a little note that said *set out the second chair* — two weeks old. He'd put the chair out two weeks ago and sat across from it, waiting for us.

"Must have, like, atrophied or something," I said. Then: "So he knew — he knew that we would start a war? And grow this."

He knew we were a wild card he could leverage. The minute he saw what we did to Romag, the weakest of the four temples decided we might be the way he survives. I understood the logic. I'd have done the same. *Which is exactly why* — "But also safety, so we should not trust it."

"Okay, but look — Kelno has troops because he controls it, right? Well, I tell them, just post some guards, to make sure no one enters the water temple. Just so we can conduct our business with the owner."

"So you can conduct your business," he said. "Alright."

I stood. The chair he'd kept warm for two weeks held my shape now.

---

## sequoia — The Captain's Report

Dren came down the processional corridor while we were going up it, which meant he'd already done the one thing we'd asked. Calmer was upstairs. Safe. Or as safe as anything in here.

He looked at me first. He always did. Some recruitment negotiation weeks back left him with the idea I was the reasonable one. I'm not. I just talk less.

"Oh my god," he said, "that guy whines a lot." A pause. "He won't shut up."

He meant Calmer. The injured cleric I'd watched bleed in three different rooms now. Dren had carried him up two flights of brown marble and the thanks he wanted was to be heard out about it.

"He's a worrier at heart," Zephyr said.

That was generous. Calmer wasn't a worrier. He just couldn't say his own god's name in here, so it came out sideways, as complaint. But Dren didn't need that, and there was no need for him to know.

Then the report.

"The captain, the captain? March is finished." Dren said it fast, the way men say things they ran to deliver. "Belsornig's people are at his door. He's screaming for you."

So. That was the war we'd built, arriving on schedule. The Fire Temple folding inward. Water Temple knives at the captain's threshold. Somewhere south of us the smell of charred gnoll had been hanging in the corridors all morning, and now it had a body count.

Dren wanted that understood — that he was reporting, not deciding. "You didn't put me in charge of mercenaries, because I tell you what to do." He shrugged, a soldier's shrug. "You tell me."

I noted it. A man who knew exactly what he was for. Rare, in this place.

"Alrrem's on his pyre," he went on. "Belsornig pulling his treasury out the south route." Then the assessment, clean as a quartermaster's ledger. "Low. Healthy. Vulnerable. Your calls."

Three temple leaders. One burning, one robbed, one screaming. We did that. Not by accident. We took bribes from the Fire Temple, sold a chimera, knifed Romag at his own table, and pointed three armies at each other until they marched. One does not simply take bribes to defect and end up the rightful lords of anything. We ended up here anyway.

Zephyr was already weighing routes, the grim arithmetic of which corpse to loot first. The captain screaming for rescue. Belsornig's treasury walking out a back door. I let him run it. He's better at the math when there's more than one prize in the room.

We moved into the next chamber. Dren came with us, and that was the problem.

"Sounds good, boss." He said it to me, of course. "What do you want me to be doing?"

There was the trap. Dren was a mercenary in a temple full of mercenaries who would all sell us for a copper if the war tilted. He wouldn't. I didn't know why. Maybe it was the negotiation. Maybe he just liked having someone tell him the truth about what he was for. Either way he was the only blade in this entire stone box that pointed our direction and meant it.

Which meant he could not come into the water tank. A loyal man is an asset. A dead loyal man is a waste. We had one.

Zephyr fumbled it out loud, the way he does when he's solving a problem in front of an audience. "You could stay here with — what's his name. Kelno." Then he caught himself. Calmer was upstairs. We took him up. "Oh. Shit."

I didn't say anything. There wasn't a need. The right answer was sitting in the room already: put Dren where the cleric was, and let the only loyal man in the temple guard the only man who couldn't run.

"Take your mercenaries and stay back," Zephyr landed on, finally.

"Take a vacation," somebody muttered.

Dren didn't argue. He didn't take orders, except from us, and that was the whole point of him. "Okay, boss." He gathered his two men off the wall, gave me one last look — the look that still thought I was the reasonable one — and they headed out, back toward the stairs, back toward Calmer.

We went the other way. Toward the water.

The dripping had started somewhere above us, in a ceiling that hadn't been dripping an hour ago.

That's not good.

---

## zinnia — Entry into the Water Temple

Dren said "okay, boss," and that was the last easy thing.

We came up on the Water Temple and the corridor opened into a hall the size of a barracks, the ceiling arched and lost somewhere in the shadow high overhead. The walls were slabs of polished stone, blue swirled into deep green, and some old magic had been laid into them — a soft, cloudy luminosity that bled out of the stone and floated in the damp air, so the whole chamber felt submerged. Like standing at the bottom of the sea. Even the air smelled of salt.

I drank it in. That is the job, before anything else: read the room. Bronze everywhere, all of it gone to verdigris, that green crust that says nobody has touched this in a long time, or nobody dares. A great sheet of bronze set into the west wall. An altar near the middle of the hall, round and squat, its concave top holding a shallow basin of water, and at the bottom of the basin — coins, a scatter of gems, seashells. An offering. South of it, in the mouth of an alcove, an idol of serpentine stone: a writhing knot of fish and eels and octopi sculpted into one horrid mass, the height of a tall man and more, with a seaweed drapery hung across the wall behind. In each corner a triangular plinth ran up toward the vaulted dark, and atop each one a bronze gargoyle squatted under its coat of green.

Four watchers in the corners. A god in the alcove. Treasure in the font.

*Touch nothing.* Not the time and place to pull for treasure. Sequoia agreed in a word — "Yep" — and that settled it. We were not here to be tempted.

"Let's sneak in," Zephyr said, and we did. This part I know. This part is breath held and footfall placed and the body made small in a room built to make you feel small. Zephyr moved the way he moves when it matters — no announcement, just presence, closing the distance before the room processed it. I moved behind him and the hall did not notice us — not the gargoyles, not whatever prayed at that altar. We crossed the whole of it and nothing stirred. Clean. *Gotcha.*

Two doors at the far end, east and west. The intelligence said the bad guys were through one of them. Zephyr wanted the west — fewer rooms to cross, less chance of stumbling into something. Sound. I'd have chosen the same. He put his ear to the door first, the way you do, listening for the thing on the other side before you give it a reason to listen back. The plan was simple: hear nothing, then break it open before they can stand. Zephyr is an assassin; if we take them unawares, the first blow is already a killing one. Surprise is the whole margin. You build it carefully and you spend it once.

So we would smash the door. Splash through it. Take them while their hands were still finding their weapons.

The trouble was the door, and the trouble was me. We sized up who had the shoulders for it. Zephyr's no good for that work, never has been. Sequoia about the same. I'm the one built to put a body through a barrier — so I set myself, and I hit it.

It didn't give.

I bounced. The door held and threw me back, and the impact went up my arm and nowhere else useful. Someone moved to help. I set again and hit it a second time, everything I had, the strongest of us against a door that wasn't even locked so much as stubborn — and it threw me again. Twice. The same flat, jarring nothing, twice.

*This amateur-hour infiltration has got to stop.* I have thought that about every one of them. Calmer can't stay quiet. Zephyr takes the risk for the flourish. And here it was my own shoulder that botched the one thing I was put on the door to do.

Behind the door, the two men went still, then loud. They'd felt it — the door shuddering, something heavy throwing itself at them twice and not getting through. They shouted a name I couldn't make out through the bronze, muffled, a question more than a command.

"You're not supposed to be here. Who is this?"

The margin was gone. We had crossed that whole stretch of glowing water-light without a sound, and lost it all on a door.

---

## zephyr — Infiltration of the Water Temple

The west door. Always the west door — fewer rooms, fewer surprises, fewer chances for a war zone to introduce me to something it had been saving. I put my ear to the brown marble and listened. Nothing. The plan was to burst in.

The plan died when Zinnia hit the door and the door hit back.

He rolled her shoulder into it and bounced. Tried again, with help, bounced again. *That was a sorry effort,* He said, which was not the door's problem so much as a confession. Two muffled voices came through the wood now, fully awake, a name shouted that I couldn't make out, then the question that ends so many infiltrations: *You're not supposed to be here? Who is this?*

Sequoia, without missing a beat: "Housekeeping!"

It did not land. I sighed. Zinnia tried informants. I tried Kelno — *We are messengers for Kelno* — and felt the thing wobble in the air, because messages from Kelno, in a temple at war with itself, is the wrong kind of password. So I changed the trade.

There was a war on. War means news, and news means doors open for the people who carry it. We were messengers, then — bearing word of an assassination, a letter, a coin to prove it. Zinnia produced the proof. The voices behind the door paused, and the pause is where you win or lose. *Yes,* one said, *and what is it?* News for the big boss. News for Belsornig.

And then they did the thing tired men do when they think they're safe — they talked. "They're dead, then, with Alrrem dying, and those fools… All that remains is for us to defeat those pompous asses known as the Obsidian Edge."

*This again.* The world had been calling us pompous behind closed doors for a while now. I could forgive it. We were about to introduce ourselves properly.

The door opened. The bluff held so clean we didn't just get in — we caught them flat-footed, the whole opening exchange ours to spend before they could lift a hand. Then they saw us, and saw the shapes wearing the wrong faces, and started to say the word — *the Obsidian Edge?* — and that was the last useful thing either of them said.

They were cult fanatics, and not entirely stupid: blades of conjured force already hanging in the air, hovering, ready for trouble. Trouble had simply come dressed as a courier.

Sequoia and Zinnia took the first one apart. The second was mine.

Surprise, for an assassin, is not an advantage. It is a sentence already finished before the man knows it started. The blade went in where it always goes. Automatic. Done.
